========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, um… thanks. I guess what brings me in is that I’ve been having a hard time shaking this feeling—well, more than a feeling, honestly—that certain people around here, mostly in the arts and finance circles in Cincinnati, are keeping track of me.

Like, not openly. It’s subtle. I work as a financial advisor, so I meet with clients, go to events, panels, receptions, that kind of thing, and it’s started to seem like some people know who I’ve been meeting with or are watching the pattern of it. Then I’ll get an invitation to something, or there’ll be a panel topic that feels oddly specific, or someone will sit near me at an event in a way that feels arranged. Little things like that. And I know, I know how it sounds—I do try to tell myself maybe it’s coincidence, maybe I’m reading into it—but I can’t really let it go once I notice it. It kind of stays with me and I keep turning it over in my head.

I don’t think so… not in the sense of, like, hearing a voice talking to me or seeing a person there that other people can’t see. I haven’t had anything like that, at least not that I’d call that.

It’s not like I’m hearing something that no one else could hear. And visually, no, I don’t think I’ve seen things that weren’t there. It’s more the patterns and the timing of things that get to me.

It’s more the patterns and the timing of things that get to me.\n\n[Last Question]\nBefore these last two months, would you say you've generally been a trusting person, or have you tended to be wary and suspicious of others' motives for most of your adult life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 32 / personal_financial_advisor / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I wouldn’t have described myself as suspicious, exactly. I mean, in finance you kind of have to be careful with people’s motives, right? You’re trained to pay attention to incentives, conflicts of interest, what someone stands to gain, whether something is being presented a certain way. So I’ve probably always been… analytical, maybe guarded in a professional sense.

But in my personal life, I don’t think I went around assuming people were against me or anything like that. I had normal doubts about people sometimes, like anyone does, but I don’t remember feeling this kind of constant need to connect the dots or figure out whether something was aimed at me. That feels more recent. At least, that’s how it seems to me now.

It’s… mostly the same on the surface, in the sense that I’m still working and I’m still handling basic day-to-day things. I’m not, you know, unable to get out of bed or completely dropping everything. But if I’m being honest, there has been some decline professionally.

My practice has gotten smaller than it was, and part of that is because I’ve been missing follow-ups or putting them off longer than I normally would. I used to be pretty consistent about staying in touch with clients, checking in, keeping the relationship warm, that kind of thing. Lately it takes more effort, and sometimes I avoid reaching out if I’m not sure how the interaction is going to feel or what might come from it.

I’ve also avoided some public panels and events I probably would have done before. Those used to be useful for networking and visibility, and I know skipping them doesn’t help my business. But I find myself backing out or not volunteering for things like that as much.

So, I wouldn’t say everything has fallen apart, but I’m not functioning at quite the same level I was. Especially with keeping up client contact and follow-through. That’s where I notice it most.

I’m not, you know, unable to get out of bed or completely dropping everything. But if I’m being honest, there has been some decline professionally.\n\nMy practice has gotten smaller than it was, and part of that is because I’ve been missing follow-ups or putting them off longer than I normally would. I used to be pretty consistent about staying in touch with clients, checking in, keeping the relationship warm, that kind of thing. Lately it takes more effort, and sometimes I avoid reaching out if I’m not sure how the interaction is going to feel or what might come from it.\n\nI’ve also avoided some public panels and events I probably would have done before. Those used to be useful for networking and visibility, and I know skipping them doesn’t help my business. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think so, though it’s a little hard for me to separate what’s emotional from what’s just being tired or preoccupied.

I’m still doing things, but it’s more in pieces. Like with work, I’ll handle the urgent things, I’ll respond when I really have to, but I don’t have the same push I used to have. Client follow-ups will just sit there longer than they should, and I’ll know I should make the call or send the email, but it feels like this extra weight to start it. Before, I was more proactive. I had more of a spark, I guess.

Outside of work, yeah, I’ve been skipping things I normally liked. I used to go to jazz nights sometimes, or play pickup basketball, and now I’ll just not go. And the weird part is I don’t always feel that disappointed about missing it. It’s not like, “Oh no, I wish I’d gone.” It’s more like… nothing. Or just, “That would take effort.”

And with friends, when they ask what’s going on, I give these short answers. Not because I’m trying to be rude, but talking feels like more effort than it should. Explaining myself feels exhausting, so I’ll say, “I’m fine,” or “Just busy,” and leave it there. I don’t know if I’d call it emotionally flat exactly, but I do feel less engaged than I usually am.

It kind of stays with me and I keep turning it over in my head.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been noticing and feeling concerned about these patterns of being watched or tracked—weeks, months, or longer?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been about two months, maybe a little over if I’m being really exact. It wasn’t like one day I woke up and thought, “Okay, this is happening.” It sort of built up.\n\nAt first it was just noticing odd overlaps—like I’d meet with a client or have a conversation, and then something at an event or in an email invitation would feel like it was pointing back to that. But around two months ago is when it started feeling consistent enough that I couldn’t just brush it off. That’s when I started really paying attention to the idea that people might be tracking who I’m meeting with and kind of sending signals through those situations.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced anything like hearing voices, sounds, or seeing things that others don't seem to notice, along with these feelings of being watched?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of, like, hearing a voice talking to me or seeing a person there that other people can’t see. I haven’t had anything like that, at least not that I’d call that.\n\nThere are times at events where I’ll notice a sound or a bit of conversation from across the room and I’ll wonder if it’s connected to me, but that’s more me interpreting what’s happening around me, I guess. It’s not like I’m hearing something that no one else could hear. And visually, no, I don’t think I’ve seen things that weren’t there. It’s more the patterns and the timing of things that get to me.\n\nDoctor: Before these last two months, would you say you've generally been a trusting person, or have you tended to be wary and suspicious of others' motives for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I wouldn’t have described myself as suspicious, exactly. I mean, in finance you kind of have to be careful with people’s motives, right? I’m not, you know, unable to get out of bed or completely dropping everything. I used to be pretty consistent about staying in touch with clients, checking in, keeping the relationship warm, that kind of thing. I had more of a spark, I guess.\n\nOutside of work, yeah, I’ve been skipping things I normally liked. I used to go to jazz nights sometimes, or play pickup basketball, and now I’ll just not go. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think maybe it did start before the suspiciousness got really noticeable. Not in a dramatic way, though. It wasn’t like one day I suddenly stopped functioning or anything. It was more like I was still doing what I needed to do, but only in pieces, if that makes sense.

Like with work, I could still handle the things that were urgent or right in front of me, but I didn’t have the same spark I used to have. I’d let client follow-ups sit longer than I normally would, and then I’d feel this sort of resistance to starting them. Not even because the task was complicated — it’s just a phone call, an email, a check-in — but it felt heavier than it should have.

And outside of work, I started skipping things I used to actually enjoy, like jazz nights or pickup basketball. Before, those were easy yeses for me, or at least things I’d look forward to. But I’d find myself just not going, and the strange part is I didn’t feel that much about missing them. It wasn’t like I was upset or guilty. It was more just… blank, or like, “Okay, I didn’t go.”

With friends too, I think I got more withdrawn. If someone asked what was going on, I’d give short answers — “I’m fine,” “just busy,” whatever. Not because I didn’t care about them, but talking felt like it took more effort than it should. So yeah, looking back, there probably were some changes before the last two months. I just didn’t really label them as anything at the time.

That’s where I notice it most.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your emotional expression or motivation—like feeling emotionally flat, losing interest in things you used to enjoy, or having less energy or drive—since this all started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, though it’s a little hard for me to separate what’s emotional from what’s just being tired or preoccupied.\n\nI’m still doing things, but it’s more in pieces. Like with work, I’ll handle the urgent things, I’ll respond when I really have to, but I don’t have the same push I used to have. Client follow-ups will just sit there longer than they should, and I’ll know I should make the call or send the email, but it feels like this extra weight to start it. Before, I was more proactive. I had more of a spark, I guess.\n\nOutside of work, yeah, I’ve been skipping things I normally liked. I used to go to jazz nights sometimes, or play pickup basketball, and now I’ll just not go. And the weird part is I don’t always feel that disappointed about missing it. It’s not like, “Oh no, I wish I’d gone.” It’s more like… nothing. Or just, “That would take effort.”\n\nAnd with friends, when they ask what’s going on, I give these short answers. Not because I’m trying to be rude, but talking feels like more effort than it should. Explaining myself feels exhausting, so I’ll say, “I’m fine,” or “Just busy,” and leave it there. I don’t know if I’d call it emotionally flat exactly, but I do feel less engaged than I usually am.\n\nDoctor: Before these suspicions started about two months ago, did you notice any earlier changes—like being less motivated, more withdrawn, or just not feeling like yourself—going back further, maybe four to six months or more?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think maybe it did start before the suspiciousness got really noticeable. Not in a dramatic way, though. It wasn’t like one day I suddenly stopped functioning or anything. It was more like I was still doing what I needed to do, but only in pieces, if that makes sense.\n\nLike with work, I could still handle the things that were urgent or right in front of me, but I didn’t have the same spark I used to have. I’d let client follow-ups sit longer than I normally would, and then I’d feel this sort of resistance to starting them. Not even because the task was complicated — it’s just a phone call, an email, a check-in — but it felt heavier than it should have.\n\nAnd outside of work, I started skipping things I used to actually enjoy, like jazz nights or pickup basketball. Before, those were easy yeses for me, or at least things I’d look forward to. But I’d find myself just not going, and the strange part is I didn’t feel that much about missing them. It wasn’t like I was upset or guilty. It was more just… blank, or like, “Okay, I didn’t go.”\n\nWith friends too, I think I got more withdrawn. If someone asked what was going on, I’d give short answers — “I’m fine,” “just busy,” whatever. Not because I didn’t care about them, but talking felt like it took more effort than it should. So yeah, looking back, there probably were some changes before the last two months. I just didn’t really label them as anything at the time.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know that I’d call it a low mood, exactly. I mean, I have moments where I feel frustrated with myself, because I know I’m not doing things the way I normally would, and then I’ll think, “Why is this so hard?” But it doesn’t feel like I’m walking around sad all day or crying or feeling hopeless in that kind of way.

It’s more like the engine isn’t turning over. Like I can see the thing I’m supposed to do, and part of me understands why it matters, but there’s not much momentum behind it. And with things I used to enjoy, it’s not always that they feel miserable or pointless — it’s more that the interest just isn’t there the same way, or it feels like too much effort to get myself started.

So… no, not persistently sad, I don’t think. More flat, maybe, or stalled. And then I get down on myself in little bursts because I’m aware of it, but I wouldn’t say the main feeling is sadness.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n8\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF20.0, F25.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome in. I'm Dr. Smith. Can you tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… thanks. I guess what brings me in is that I’ve been having a hard time shaking this feeling—well, more than a feeling, honestly—that certain people around here, mostly in the arts and finance circles in Cincinnati, are keeping track of me.\n\nLike, not openly. It’s subtle. I work as a financial advisor, so I meet with clients, go to events, panels, receptions, that kind of thing, and it’s started to seem like some people know who I’ve been meeting with or are watching the pattern of it. Then I’ll get an invitation to something, or there’ll be a panel topic that feels oddly specific, or someone will sit near me at an event in a way that feels arranged. Little things like that. And I know, I know how it sounds—I do try to tell myself maybe it’s coincidence, maybe I’m reading into it—but I can’t really let it go once I notice it. It kind of stays with me and I keep turning it over in my head.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been noticing and feeling concerned about these patterns of being watched or tracked—weeks, months, or longer?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been about two months, maybe a little over if I’m being really exact. It wasn’t like one day I woke up and thought, “Okay, this is happening.” It sort of built up.\n\nAt first it was just noticing odd overlaps—like I’d meet with a client or have a conversation, and then something at an event or in an email invitation would feel like it was pointing back to that. But around two months ago is when it started feeling consistent enough that I couldn’t just brush it off. That’s when I started really paying attention to the idea that people might be tracking who I’m meeting with and kind of sending signals through those situations.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced anything like hearing voices, sounds, or seeing things that others don't seem to notice, along with these feelings of being watched?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of, like, hearing a voice talking to me or seeing a person there that other people can’t see. I haven’t had anything like that, at least not that I’d call that.\n\nThere are times at events where I’ll notice a sound or a bit of conversation from across the room and I’ll wonder if it’s connected to me, but that’s more me interpreting what’s happening around me, I guess. It’s not like I’m hearing something that no one else could hear. And visually, no, I don’t think I’ve seen things that weren’t there. It’s more the patterns and the timing of things that get to me.\n\nDoctor: Before these last two months, would you say you've generally been a trusting person, or have you tended to be wary and suspicious of others' motives for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I wouldn’t have described myself as suspicious, exactly. I mean, in finance you kind of have to be careful with people’s motives, right? You’re trained to pay attention to incentives, conflicts of interest, what someone stands to gain, whether something is being presented a certain way. So I’ve probably always been… analytical, maybe guarded in a professional sense.\n\nBut in my personal life, I don’t think I went around assuming people were against me or anything like that. I had normal doubts about people sometimes, like anyone does, but I don’t remember feeling this kind of constant need to connect the dots or figure out whether something was aimed at me. That feels more recent. At least, that’s how it seems to me now.\n\nDoctor: Aside from these suspicions, has your ability to do your job, manage daily responsibilities, and interact with family or friends stayed pretty much the same as before, or have you noticed a decline in how you're functioning overall?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly the same on the surface, in the sense that I’m still working and I’m still handling basic day-to-day things. I’m not, you know, unable to get out of bed or completely dropping everything. But if I’m being honest, there has been some decline professionally.\n\nMy practice has gotten smaller than it was, and part of that is because I’ve been missing follow-ups or putting them off longer than I normally would. I used to be pretty consistent about staying in touch with clients, checking in, keeping the relationship warm, that kind of thing. Lately it takes more effort, and sometimes I avoid reaching out if I’m not sure how the interaction is going to feel or what might come from it.\n\nI’ve also avoided some public panels and events I probably would have done before. Those used to be useful for networking and visibility, and I know skipping them doesn’t help my business. But I find myself backing out or not volunteering for things like that as much.\n\nSo, I wouldn’t say everything has fallen apart, but I’m not functioning at quite the same level I was. Especially with keeping up client contact and follow-through. That’s where I notice it most.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your emotional expression or motivation—like feeling emotionally flat, losing interest in things you used to enjoy, or having less energy or drive—since this all started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, though it’s a little hard for me to separate what’s emotional from what’s just being tired or preoccupied.\n\nI’m still doing things, but it’s more in pieces. Like with work, I’ll handle the urgent things, I’ll respond when I really have to, but I don’t have the same push I used to have. Client follow-ups will just sit there longer than they should, and I’ll know I should make the call or send the email, but it feels like this extra weight to start it. Before, I was more proactive. I had more of a spark, I guess.\n\nOutside of work, yeah, I’ve been skipping things I normally liked. I used to go to jazz nights sometimes, or play pickup basketball, and now I’ll just not go. And the weird part is I don’t always feel that disappointed about missing it. It’s not like, “Oh no, I wish I’d gone.” It’s more like… nothing. Or just, “That would take effort.”\n\nAnd with friends, when they ask what’s going on, I give these short answers. Not because I’m trying to be rude, but talking feels like more effort than it should. Explaining myself feels exhausting, so I’ll say, “I’m fine,” or “Just busy,” and leave it there. I don’t know if I’d call it emotionally flat exactly, but I do feel less engaged than I usually am.\n\nDoctor: Before these suspicions started about two months ago, did you notice any earlier changes—like being less motivated, more withdrawn, or just not feeling like yourself—going back further, maybe four to six months or more?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think maybe it did start before the suspiciousness got really noticeable. Not in a dramatic way, though. It wasn’t like one day I suddenly stopped functioning or anything. It was more like I was still doing what I needed to do, but only in pieces, if that makes sense.\n\nLike with work, I could still handle the things that were urgent or right in front of me, but I didn’t have the same spark I used to have. I’d let client follow-ups sit longer than I normally would, and then I’d feel this sort of resistance to starting them. Not even because the task was complicated — it’s just a phone call, an email, a check-in — but it felt heavier than it should have.\n\nAnd outside of work, I started skipping things I used to actually enjoy, like jazz nights or pickup basketball. Before, those were easy yeses for me, or at least things I’d look forward to. But I’d find myself just not going, and the strange part is I didn’t feel that much about missing them. It wasn’t like I was upset or guilty. It was more just… blank, or like, “Okay, I didn’t go.”\n\nWith friends too, I think I got more withdrawn. If someone asked what was going on, I’d give short answers — “I’m fine,” “just busy,” whatever. Not because I didn’t care about them, but talking felt like it took more effort than it should. So yeah, looking back, there probably were some changes before the last two months. I just didn’t really label them as anything at the time.\n\nDoctor: Along with this drop in motivation and interest, have you also been feeling persistently sad, hopeless, or down on yourself—like an actual low mood—or is it more just a lack of drive without that emotional sadness?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it a low mood, exactly. I mean, I have moments where I feel frustrated with myself, because I know I’m not doing things the way I normally would, and then I’ll think, “Why is this so hard?” But it doesn’t feel like I’m walking around sad all day or crying or feeling hopeless in that kind of way.\n\nIt’s more like the engine isn’t turning over. Like I can see the thing I’m supposed to do, and part of me understands why it matters, but there’s not much momentum behind it. And with things I used to enjoy, it’s not always that they feel miserable or pointless — it’s more that the interest just isn’t there the same way, or it feels like too much effort to get myself started.\n\nSo… no, not persistently sad, I don’t think. More flat, maybe, or stalled. And then I get down on myself in little bursts because I’m aware of it, but I wouldn’t say the main feeling is sadness.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your emotional expression or motivation—like feeling emotionally flat, losing interest in things you used to enjoy, or having less energy or drive—since this all started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, though it’s a little hard for me to separate what’s emotional from what’s just being tired or preoccupied.\n\nI’m still doing things, but it’s more in pieces. Client follow-ups will just sit there longer than they should, and I’ll know I should make the call or send the email, but it feels like this extra weight to start it. Before, I was more proactive. I had more of a spark, I guess.\n\nOutside of work, yeah, I’ve been skipping things I normally liked. I used to go to jazz nights sometimes, or play pickup basketball, and now I’ll just not go. And the weird part is I don’t always feel that disappointed about missing it. It’s not like, “Oh no, I wish I’d gone.” It’s more like… nothing. Or just, “That would take effort.”\n\nAnd with friends, when they ask what’s going on, I give these short answers. Not because I’m trying to be rude, but talking feels like more effort than it should. Explaining myself feels exhausting, so I’ll say, “I’m fine,” or “Just busy,” and leave it there. I don’t know if I’d call it emotionally flat exactly, but I do feel less engaged than I usually am.\n\nDoctor: Before these suspicions started about two months ago, did you notice any earlier changes—like being less motivated, more withdrawn, or just not feeling like yourself—going back further, maybe four to six months or more?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think maybe it did start before the suspiciousness got really noticeable. Not in a dramatic way, though. It wasn’t like one day I suddenly stopped functioning or anything. It was more like I was still doing what I needed to do, but only in pieces, if that makes sense.\n\nLike with work, I could still handle the things that were urgent or right in front of me, but I didn’t have the same spark I used to have. I’d let client follow-ups sit longer than I normally would, and then I’d feel this sort of resistance to starting them. Not even because the task was complicated — it’s just a phone call, an email, a check-in — but it felt heavier than it should have.\n\nAnd outside of work, I started skipping things I used to actually enjoy, like jazz nights or pickup basketball. Before, those were easy yeses for me, or at least things I’d look forward to. But I’d find myself just not going, and the strange part is I didn’t feel that much about missing them. It wasn’t like I was upset or guilty. It was more just… blank, or like, “Okay, I didn’t go.”\n\nWith friends too, I think I got more withdrawn. If someone asked what was going on, I’d give short answers — “I’m fine,” “just busy,” whatever. Not because I didn’t care about them, but talking felt like it took more effort than it should. So yeah, looking back, there probably were some changes before the last two months. I just didn’t really label them as anything at the time.\n\nDoctor: Along with this drop in motivation and interest, have you also been feeling persistently sad, hopeless, or down on yourself—like an actual low mood—or is it more just a lack of drive without that emotional sadness?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it a low mood, exactly. I mean, I have moments where I feel frustrated with myself, because I know I’m not doing things the way I normally would, and then I’ll think, “Why is this so hard?” But it doesn’t feel like I’m walking around sad all day or crying or feeling hopeless in that kind of way.\n\nIt’s more like the engine isn’t turning over. Like I can see the thing I’m supposed to do, and part of me understands why it matters, but there’s not much momentum behind it. And with things I used to enjoy, it’s not always that they feel miserable or pointless — it’s more that the interest just isn’t there the same way, or it feels like too much effort to get myself started.\n\nSo… no, not persistently sad, I don’t think. More flat, maybe, or stalled. And then I get down on myself in little bursts because I’m aware of it, but I wouldn’t say the main feeling is sadness.\n\n[Last Question]\nThinking back, would you say these changes in your motivation, energy, and interest in activities have been going on fairly continuously for six months or more, or did they come and go during that time?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 32 / personal_financial_advisor / bachelors\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"These changes have been building since that arts nonprofit recognition a little over two years ago.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Honestly, looking back, I think it’s been more continuous than I wanted to admit. Not exactly the same intensity the whole time, but it’s been building for a long time — probably since that arts nonprofit recognition thing a little over two years ago.

At the time I didn’t think of it as “motivation problems” or anything like that. I just thought maybe I was tired, or maybe that whole period took more out of me than I realized. But after that, little by little, I started feeling less like myself. I was still functioning, still working, still showing up enough that from the outside it probably didn’t look that dramatic. But the extra drive, the initiative, the wanting to do things — that started fading.

It would come in waves a little, where some weeks I could push through better than others, but I don’t think it fully went away. It’s more like there’s been this baseline of lower energy and less interest that’s been there, and then recently it’s become more obvious because I’m noticing how much I’m avoiding or putting off. So yeah… not just the last few months. I’d say it’s been building pretty steadily for over two years.

I’m not, you know, unable to get out of bed or completely dropping everything. I’m not really sure. I mean, I don’t think I’ve noticed anything like my speech being… totally hard to follow or me bouncing from one subject to something completely unrelated. At least nobody has sat me down and said, “You’re not making sense,” or anything that direct.

I'm Dr. Smith. Can you tell me what brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… thanks. I guess what brings me in is that I’ve been having a hard time shaking this feeling—well, more than a feeling, honestly—that certain people around here, mostly in the arts and finance circles in Cincinnati, are keeping track of me.\n\nLike, not openly. It’s subtle. I work as a financial advisor, so I meet with clients, go to events, panels, receptions, that kind of thing, and it’s started to seem like some people know who I’ve been meeting with or are watching the pattern of it. Then I’ll get an invitation to something, or there’ll be a panel topic that feels oddly specific, or someone will sit near me at an event in a way that feels arranged. Little things like that. It kind of stays with me and I keep turning it over in my head.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been noticing and feeling concerned about these patterns of being watched or tracked—weeks, months, or longer?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been about two months, maybe a little over if I’m being really exact. It wasn’t like one day I woke up and thought, “Okay, this is happening.” It sort of built up.\n\nAt first it was just noticing odd overlaps—like I’d meet with a client or have a conversation, and then something at an event or in an email invitation would feel like it was pointing back to that. But around two months ago is when it started feeling consistent enough that I couldn’t just brush it off. That’s when I started really paying attention to the idea that people might be tracking who I’m meeting with and kind of sending signals through those situations.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced anything like hearing voices, sounds, or seeing things that others don't seem to notice, along with these feelings of being watched?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of, like, hearing a voice talking to me or seeing a person there that other people can’t see. I haven’t had anything like that, at least not that I’d call that.\n\nThere are times at events where I’ll notice a sound or a bit of conversation from across the room and I’ll wonder if it’s connected to me, but that’s more me interpreting what’s happening around me, I guess. It’s not like I’m hearing something that no one else could hear. And visually, no, I don’t think I’ve seen things that weren’t there. It’s more the patterns and the timing of things that get to me.\n\nDoctor: Before these last two months, would you say you've generally been a trusting person, or have you tended to be wary and suspicious of others' motives for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I wouldn’t have described myself as suspicious, exactly. I mean, in finance you kind of have to be careful with people’s motives, right? You’re trained to pay attention to incentives, conflicts of interest, what someone stands to gain, whether something is being presented a certain way. So I’ve probably always been… analytical, maybe guarded in a professional sense.\n\nBut in my personal life, I don’t think I went around assuming people were against me or anything like that. I had normal doubts about people sometimes, like anyone does, but I don’t remember feeling this kind of constant need to connect the dots or figure out whether something was aimed at me. That feels more recent. At least, that’s how it seems to me now.\n\nDoctor: Aside from these suspicions, has your ability to do your job, manage daily responsibilities, and interact with family or friends stayed pretty much the same as before, or have you noticed a decline in how you're functioning overall?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly the same on the surface, in the sense that I’m still working and I’m still handling basic day-to-day things. I’m not, you know, unable to get out of bed or completely dropping everything. But if I’m being honest, there has been some decline professionally.\n\nMy practice has gotten smaller than it was, and part of that is because I’ve been missing follow-ups or putting them off longer than I normally would. I used to be pretty consistent about staying in touch with clients, checking in, keeping the relationship warm, that kind of thing. Lately it takes more effort, and sometimes I avoid reaching out if I’m not sure how the interaction is going to feel or what might come from it.\n\nI’ve also avoided some public panels and events I probably would have done before. Those used to be useful for networking and visibility, and I know skipping them doesn’t help my business. But I find myself backing out or not volunteering for things like that as much.\n\nSo, I wouldn’t say everything has fallen apart, but I’m not functioning at quite the same level I was. Especially with keeping up client contact and follow-through. That’s where I notice it most.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your emotional expression or motivation—like feeling emotionally flat, losing interest in things you used to enjoy, or having less energy or drive—since this all started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, though it’s a little hard for me to separate what’s emotional from what’s just being tired or preoccupied.\n\nI’m still doing things, but it’s more in pieces. Like with work, I’ll handle the urgent things, I’ll respond when I really have to, but I don’t have the same push I used to have. Client follow-ups will just sit there longer than they should, and I’ll know I should make the call or send the email, but it feels like this extra weight to start it. Before, I was more proactive. I had more of a spark, I guess.\n\nOutside of work, yeah, I’ve been skipping things I normally liked. I used to go to jazz nights sometimes, or play pickup basketball, and now I’ll just not go. And the weird part is I don’t always feel that disappointed about missing it. It’s not like, “Oh no, I wish I’d gone.” It’s more like… nothing. Or just, “That would take effort.”\n\nAnd with friends, when they ask what’s going on, I give these short answers. Not because I’m trying to be rude, but talking feels like more effort than it should. Explaining myself feels exhausting, so I’ll say, “I’m fine,” or “Just busy,” and leave it there. I don’t know if I’d call it emotionally flat exactly, but I do feel less engaged than I usually am.\n\nDoctor: Before these suspicions started about two months ago, did you notice any earlier changes—like being less motivated, more withdrawn, or just not feeling like yourself—going back further, maybe four to six months or more?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think maybe it did start before the suspiciousness got really noticeable. Not in a dramatic way, though. It wasn’t like one day I suddenly stopped functioning or anything. It was more like I was still doing what I needed to do, but only in pieces, if that makes sense.\n\nLike with work, I could still handle the things that were urgent or right in front of me, but I didn’t have the same spark I used to have. I’d let client follow-ups sit longer than I normally would, and then I’d feel this sort of resistance to starting them. Not even because the task was complicated — it’s just a phone call, an email, a check-in — but it felt heavier than it should have.\n\nAnd outside of work, I started skipping things I used to actually enjoy, like jazz nights or pickup basketball. Before, those were easy yeses for me, or at least things I’d look forward to. But I’d find myself just not going, and the strange part is I didn’t feel that much about missing them. It wasn’t like I was upset or guilty. It was more just… blank, or like, “Okay, I didn’t go.”\n\nWith friends too, I think I got more withdrawn. If someone asked what was going on, I’d give short answers — “I’m fine,” “just busy,” whatever. Not because I didn’t care about them, but talking felt like it took more effort than it should. So yeah, looking back, there probably were some changes before the last two months. I just didn’t really label them as anything at the time.\n\nDoctor: Along with this drop in motivation and interest, have you also been feeling persistently sad, hopeless, or down on yourself—like an actual low mood—or is it more just a lack of drive without that emotional sadness?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it a low mood, exactly. I mean, I have moments where I feel frustrated with myself, because I know I’m not doing things the way I normally would, and then I’ll think, “Why is this so hard?” But it doesn’t feel like I’m walking around sad all day or crying or feeling hopeless in that kind of way.\n\nIt’s more like the engine isn’t turning over. Like I can see the thing I’m supposed to do, and part of me understands why it matters, but there’s not much momentum behind it. And with things I used to enjoy, it’s not always that they feel miserable or pointless — it’s more that the interest just isn’t there the same way, or it feels like too much effort to get myself started.\n\nSo… no, not persistently sad, I don’t think. More flat, maybe, or stalled. And then I get down on myself in little bursts because I’m aware of it, but I wouldn’t say the main feeling is sadness.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back, would you say these changes in your motivation, energy, and interest in activities have been going on fairly continuously for six months or more, or did they come and go during that time?\n\nPatient: Honestly, looking back, I think it’s been more continuous than I wanted to admit. Not exactly the same intensity the whole time, but it’s been building for a long time — probably since that arts nonprofit recognition thing a little over two years ago.\n\nAt the time I didn’t think of it as “motivation problems” or anything like that. So yeah… not just the last few months. I’d say it’s been building pretty steadily for over two years.\n\nDoctor: Have you or people close to you noticed any changes in how you organize your thoughts or express yourself—like your speech becoming hard to follow, jumping between unrelated topics, or your behavior seeming unusual or disorganized to others?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not really in the sense of, like, my thoughts being read or controlled. I don’t think someone is putting thoughts in my head or broadcasting them or anything like that. And I don’t think I have some special power.

It’s more specific than that, and it’s part of why I came in, honestly. For a while now I’ve had this conviction that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are sort of… quietly tracking me. Not tracking like with a device necessarily, but watching which clients I meet with, who I’m connected to, where I show up. And then I’ll notice things that feel like signals — like an invitation arriving right after I meet with someone, or a panel topic that seems too closely connected to something I just discussed, or even who ends up sitting near me at an event.

And I know how that sounds. I can say to myself, “Okay, maybe that’s just coincidence, maybe you’re reading into it.” But it doesn’t really settle. I can’t quite let it go, because the timing and the pattern feel too specific sometimes. It’s like there’s this layer of communication happening around me that nobody is saying directly, but I’m supposed to notice it.

And I know, I know how it sounds—I do try to tell myself maybe it’s coincidence, maybe I’m reading into it—but I can’t really let it go once I notice it. It kind of stays with me and I keep turning it over in my head.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been noticing and feeling concerned about these patterns of being watched or tracked—weeks, months, or longer?\n\nPatient: I’d say it’s been about two months, maybe a little over if I’m being really exact. It wasn’t like one day I woke up and thought, “Okay, this is happening.” It sort of built up.\n\nAt first it was just noticing odd overlaps—like I’d meet with a client or have a conversation, and then something at an event or in an email invitation would feel like it was pointing back to that. But around two months ago is when it started feeling consistent enough that I couldn’t just brush it off. That’s when I started really paying attention to the idea that people might be tracking who I’m meeting with and kind of sending signals through those situations.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced anything like hearing voices, sounds, or seeing things that others don't seem to notice, along with these feelings of being watched?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the sense of, like, hearing a voice talking to me or seeing a person there that other people can’t see. I haven’t had anything like that, at least not that I’d call that.\n\nThere are times at events where I’ll notice a sound or a bit of conversation from across the room and I’ll wonder if it’s connected to me, but that’s more me interpreting what’s happening around me, I guess. It’s not like I’m hearing something that no one else could hear. And visually, no, I don’t think I’ve seen things that weren’t there. It’s more the patterns and the timing of things that get to me.\n\nDoctor: Before these last two months, would you say you've generally been a trusting person, or have you tended to be wary and suspicious of others' motives for most of your adult life?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, because I wouldn’t have described myself as suspicious, exactly. I mean, in finance you kind of have to be careful with people’s motives, right? You’re trained to pay attention to incentives, conflicts of interest, what someone stands to gain, whether something is being presented a certain way. So I’ve probably always been… analytical, maybe guarded in a professional sense.\n\nBut in my personal life, I don’t think I went around assuming people were against me or anything like that. I had normal doubts about people sometimes, like anyone does, but I don’t remember feeling this kind of constant need to connect the dots or figure out whether something was aimed at me. That feels more recent. At least, that’s how it seems to me now.\n\nDoctor: Aside from these suspicions, has your ability to do your job, manage daily responsibilities, and interact with family or friends stayed pretty much the same as before, or have you noticed a decline in how you're functioning overall?\n\nPatient: It’s… mostly the same on the surface, in the sense that I’m still working and I’m still handling basic day-to-day things. I’m not, you know, unable to get out of bed or completely dropping everything. But if I’m being honest, there has been some decline professionally.\n\nMy practice has gotten smaller than it was, and part of that is because I’ve been missing follow-ups or putting them off longer than I normally would. I used to be pretty consistent about staying in touch with clients, checking in, keeping the relationship warm, that kind of thing. Lately it takes more effort, and sometimes I avoid reaching out if I’m not sure how the interaction is going to feel or what might come from it.\n\nI’ve also avoided some public panels and events I probably would have done before. Those used to be useful for networking and visibility, and I know skipping them doesn’t help my business. But I find myself backing out or not volunteering for things like that as much.\n\nSo, I wouldn’t say everything has fallen apart, but I’m not functioning at quite the same level I was. Especially with keeping up client contact and follow-through. That’s where I notice it most.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any changes in your emotional expression or motivation—like feeling emotionally flat, losing interest in things you used to enjoy, or having less energy or drive—since this all started?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, though it’s a little hard for me to separate what’s emotional from what’s just being tired or preoccupied.\n\nI’m still doing things, but it’s more in pieces. Like with work, I’ll handle the urgent things, I’ll respond when I really have to, but I don’t have the same push I used to have. Client follow-ups will just sit there longer than they should, and I’ll know I should make the call or send the email, but it feels like this extra weight to start it. Before, I was more proactive. I had more of a spark, I guess.\n\nOutside of work, yeah, I’ve been skipping things I normally liked. I used to go to jazz nights sometimes, or play pickup basketball, and now I’ll just not go. And the weird part is I don’t always feel that disappointed about missing it. It’s not like, “Oh no, I wish I’d gone.” It’s more like… nothing. Or just, “That would take effort.”\n\nAnd with friends, when they ask what’s going on, I give these short answers. Not because I’m trying to be rude, but talking feels like more effort than it should. Explaining myself feels exhausting, so I’ll say, “I’m fine,” or “Just busy,” and leave it there. I don’t know if I’d call it emotionally flat exactly, but I do feel less engaged than I usually am.\n\nDoctor: Before these suspicions started about two months ago, did you notice any earlier changes—like being less motivated, more withdrawn, or just not feeling like yourself—going back further, maybe four to six months or more?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think maybe it did start before the suspiciousness got really noticeable. Not in a dramatic way, though. It wasn’t like one day I suddenly stopped functioning or anything. It was more like I was still doing what I needed to do, but only in pieces, if that makes sense.\n\nLike with work, I could still handle the things that were urgent or right in front of me, but I didn’t have the same spark I used to have. I’d let client follow-ups sit longer than I normally would, and then I’d feel this sort of resistance to starting them. Not even because the task was complicated — it’s just a phone call, an email, a check-in — but it felt heavier than it should have.\n\nAnd outside of work, I started skipping things I used to actually enjoy, like jazz nights or pickup basketball. Before, those were easy yeses for me, or at least things I’d look forward to. But I’d find myself just not going, and the strange part is I didn’t feel that much about missing them. It wasn’t like I was upset or guilty. It was more just… blank, or like, “Okay, I didn’t go.”\n\nWith friends too, I think I got more withdrawn. If someone asked what was going on, I’d give short answers — “I’m fine,” “just busy,” whatever. Not because I didn’t care about them, but talking felt like it took more effort than it should. So yeah, looking back, there probably were some changes before the last two months. I just didn’t really label them as anything at the time.\n\nDoctor: Along with this drop in motivation and interest, have you also been feeling persistently sad, hopeless, or down on yourself—like an actual low mood—or is it more just a lack of drive without that emotional sadness?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it a low mood, exactly. I mean, I have moments where I feel frustrated with myself, because I know I’m not doing things the way I normally would, and then I’ll think, “Why is this so hard?” But it doesn’t feel like I’m walking around sad all day or crying or feeling hopeless in that kind of way.\n\nIt’s more like the engine isn’t turning over. Like I can see the thing I’m supposed to do, and part of me understands why it matters, but there’s not much momentum behind it. And with things I used to enjoy, it’s not always that they feel miserable or pointless — it’s more that the interest just isn’t there the same way, or it feels like too much effort to get myself started.\n\nSo… no, not persistently sad, I don’t think. More flat, maybe, or stalled. And then I get down on myself in little bursts because I’m aware of it, but I wouldn’t say the main feeling is sadness.\n\nDoctor: Thinking back, would you say these changes in your motivation, energy, and interest in activities have been going on fairly continuously for six months or more, or did they come and go during that time?\n\nPatient: Honestly, looking back, I think it’s been more continuous than I wanted to admit. Not exactly the same intensity the whole time, but it’s been building for a long time — probably since that arts nonprofit recognition thing a little over two years ago.\n\nAt the time I didn’t think of it as “motivation problems” or anything like that. I just thought maybe I was tired, or maybe that whole period took more out of me than I realized. But after that, little by little, I started feeling less like myself. I was still functioning, still working, still showing up enough that from the outside it probably didn’t look that dramatic. But the extra drive, the initiative, the wanting to do things — that started fading.\n\nIt would come in waves a little, where some weeks I could push through better than others, but I don’t think it fully went away. It’s more like there’s been this baseline of lower energy and less interest that’s been there, and then recently it’s become more obvious because I’m noticing how much I’m avoiding or putting off. So yeah… not just the last few months. I’d say it’s been building pretty steadily for over two years.\n\nDoctor: Have you or people close to you noticed any changes in how you organize your thoughts or express yourself—like your speech becoming hard to follow, jumping between unrelated topics, or your behavior seeming unusual or disorganized to others?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I mean, I don’t think I’ve noticed anything like my speech being… totally hard to follow or me bouncing from one subject to something completely unrelated. At least nobody has sat me down and said, “You’re not making sense,” or anything that direct.\n\nSometimes I probably explain things in a more roundabout way than I need to, especially if I’m trying to justify why I did or didn’t do something. Like I’ll give a lot of background, and then halfway through I realize maybe the other person just asked a simple question. But I’ve kind of always been someone who explains too much, especially at work, because I’m used to walking clients through details. So I don’t know if that’s a change or just me.\n\nAs far as behavior seeming disorganized… I don’t think so? I might be more scattered with following through on things, like starting something and then delaying it, but that feels more like difficulty getting myself moving than not knowing what I’m doing. My thoughts can feel cluttered sometimes, but I can usually get them across if I slow down. So no, I wouldn’t say people have told me I’m talking strangely or acting really disorganized.\n\nDoctor: Besides the feeling of being watched or tracked, have you had any other unusual beliefs or experiences—like feeling your thoughts are being read, controlled, or broadcast, or that you have some special importance or ability that others don't recognize?\n\nPatient: Not really in the sense of, like, my thoughts being read or controlled. I don’t think someone is putting thoughts in my head or broadcasting them or anything like that. And I don’t think I have some special power.\n\nIt’s more specific than that, and it’s part of why I came in, honestly. For a while now I’ve had this conviction that certain people in the arts and finance circles here in Cincinnati are sort of… quietly tracking me. Not tracking like with a device necessarily, but watching which clients I meet with, who I’m connected to, where I show up. And then I’ll notice things that feel like signals — like an invitation arriving right after I meet with someone, or a panel topic that seems too closely connected to something I just discussed, or even who ends up sitting near me at an event.\n\nAnd I know how that sounds. I can say to myself, “Okay, maybe that’s just coincidence, maybe you’re reading into it.” But it doesn’t really settle. I can’t quite let it go, because the timing and the pattern feel too specific sometimes. It’s like there’s this layer of communication happening around me that nobody is saying directly, but I’m supposed to notice it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }

This pattern—prominent negative symptoms plus active-phase delusions lasting more than a month, with clear functional decline—fits the broader symptom and duration criteria for schizophrenia rather than an isolated delusional disorder, which requires delusions without other schizophrenia-spectrum features like negative symptoms or significant functional deterioration. F22 is therefore no longer supported.", "is_final": true}

    "duration_verified": "Active delusional symptoms present for approximately 2 months; prodromal negative symptoms (avolition, anhedonia, withdrawal) present continuously for over 2 years, satisfying the 6-month overall disturbance duration criterion for schizophrenia.",
    "functional_impairment": true,
    "traumatic_stressor": null,
    "psychosocial_stressor": null,
    "additional_requirements": [
      "No hallucinations confirmed",
      "No disorganized speech or grossly disorganized/catatonic behavior confirmed",
      "Mood symptoms insufficient in severity/duration to qualify for major depressive or manic episode, ruling out schizoaffective disorder",
      "Absence of substance use or general medical condition reported as cause of symptoms",
      "Premorbid personality described as analytical/guarded but not overtly suspicious, supporting new onset of psychotic symptoms rather than longstanding paranoid personality pattern"
    ]
  }
}

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\n{\"key\":\"S019\",\"name\":\"Delusions\",\"description\":\"Fixed beliefs that do not change easily, even in the face of contradictory evidence.\",\"reason\":\"This is the first reply of the consultation, so it should return the chief complaint symptom code, which is S019.\"}\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]

Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nYeah, um… thanks.   }

Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt. Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions). Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.
